## Needs a decision

These are questions, not defects. A ruling moves to `DECISIONS.md` and closes the
row here.

| Id | Area | Question | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| <a id="fjs-d121"></a>FJS-D121 | litestone · sierra | **Does a per-caller ORDER belong in the schema — the third value-set axis?** Axis 3 of `IDEAS/value-sets.md`, held out of `FJS-D120` deliberately and WANTED, not parked. The case is the one this whole line of work started from: assigning a task, a person picks the same one to three people over and over, and an alphabetical list makes them search every time. Float the ones they actually use. Mozilla calls the general shape *frecency*; a sliding window gives decay for free. **It is not membership and must not be fused with strength.** A *suggested* binding and a *suggested* order are different sentences, and a system with one word for both cannot say *anyone may be assigned, but show me the three I actually use*. Nothing about order changes what is legal. **What makes it a question rather than a build**: it needs somewhere to KEEP the recency, and that is per-caller state rather than a fact about the data — so unlike membership and strength it is not obviously a schema declaration at all. Three shapes were considered: a `Recent` model (its own table, `Int @id` — see `packages/litestone/docs/modelling.md`), a JSON field on `User`, and localStorage. The argument FOR declaring it here anyway is the one that put the control table in one module: a hand-written picker and a generated one must not order differently. The argument AGAINST is that it is per-caller and non-authoritative, which is the definition of a UI-realm concern. A ruling has to say where the counter lives, whether the schema names it at all, and whether `resource.options()` applies it or a control does. | [value-sets.md](IDEAS/value-sets.md) § open questions · [FJS-D120](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d120) |
| <a id="fjs-d122"></a>FJS-D122 | litestone · sierra | **How does a DEPENDENT set compose — one whose members depend on another field's value?** The second axis held out of `FJS-D120`, and WANTED. Pick a country, then the state list is that country's. Pick a category, then the subcategory list narrows. Salesforce calls it a controlling field, Frappe a Dynamic Link. **The prior art is a warning rather than a model**: dependent picklists are the one feature Salesforce could not compose with global value sets, which is the exact shape `FJS-D120` just adopted — so taking their mechanism wholesale imports the incompatibility. Two halves and they may want different answers. The DATA half is a `where` that reads a sibling column, which the `@@allow` expression language can nearly say already (`@@scope` is a predicate over the row, and `@derived` reads this row's own columns) — the open part is whether the sibling being read is the value ON the record or the value currently in the FORM, which are different rows on a create. The UI half is a picker that reloads when its controlling field changes and clears a value the new list no longer contains, which touches `resource.options()`'s cache key and `$context.form`. A ruling has to say whether the dependency is declared on the set or on the binding, and what happens to a stored value that a later change to the controlling field makes illegal — refuse the write, clear the field, or leave it and report. | [value-sets.md](IDEAS/value-sets.md) § open questions · [FJS-D120](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d120) |
| <a id="fjs-d119"></a>FJS-D119 | repo | **Should every package run under Bun, or is the node/bun split load-bearing?** Today it is mixed and the mix is not documented as a choice: `ui` runs five node phases, `jetty` ten, `scripts/ci.mjs` is plain node, while `litestone` is `bun test`, `css` is `bun test/run.js`, `toolbelt` is its own harness and `sierra` is vitest. **Measured 2026-08-22: almost none of it is a constraint.** `ui/test/browser/run.mjs`, `jetty/test/phase0.test.js` and `css/test/run.js` use no `Bun.*` and no `bun:` import between them — `css`'s harness even carries `#!/usr/bin/env bun` while `CLAUDE.md`'s runner table lists it as *node driving headless Chrome*, so the two disagree about a harness that would run under either. The one genuine binding is **junction, which is Bun-only and cannot be otherwise** — `Bun.serve` is the transport, `Bun.file` is logging and static, `bun:sqlite` is cache and database. So the question is not *can we*, it is *what does the split buy*. The argument FOR keeping node: `bun run ci` is documented as plain node precisely so CI has no bun-specific behaviour in the harness itself and runs identically on a laptop — a claim worth keeping or worth retiring deliberately, not by drift. The argument AGAINST: two runtimes is two sets of traps, and `CLAUDE.md` already carries a hazard about one of them (`bun test` vs `bun run test` picking up ~35 runner artefacts in mesa) that only exists because both are in play. **What a ruling has to cover**: the runner table in `CLAUDE.md`, `scripts/ci.mjs`'s own entry point, and whether *plain node for CI* is a property being defended or an accident. Raised because a session used node exactly where the package scripts do and it was not obvious from the outside that this was by design. | — |
| <a id="fjs-d118"></a>FJS-D118 | sierra | **Does a page state where *back* goes, and if so is it an attribute?** The sibling of [FJS-D115](#fjs-d115) and deliberately not ruled with it. `KOBAMI/my.maid.tech` writes `data-return` in the same shape as `data-confirm` — an attribute, one delegated listener — to stash the path a create or edit page should return to, so a form reached from three places goes back to the right one. The mechanism looks identical and the OWNER is not: a confirmation is a UI affordance and belongs to the kit, while *where does back go* is navigation, which is `@frontierjs/sierra`'s router — the only thing that knows the history stack, the route table and whether the previous entry is even this app. Putting it on the kit's listener would make `@frontierjs/ui` reach for a router it does not depend on. What a ruling has to settle: whether the state lives in `history.state` (survives a reload, dies on a fresh tab), in the URL as a `?return=` (shareable, and a caller-supplied path is an open redirect the moment it is followed without checking), or in a store (cheapest, gone on reload); and whether an attribute is the right surface at all when the router could answer it from the route table without anybody writing anything | [IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md](IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md) § 3.1 · [FJS-D115](#fjs-d115) · [sierra router](packages/sierra/src/router/index.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d117"></a>FJS-D117 | sierra · cli | **Does a co-located route part carry its folder in its NAME, and does `fli check` grade it?** Sierra already rules the roles — `index.mesa` routes, `_module.mesa` is the layout, `_prefix` and PascalCase are co-located components — and says nothing about what those components are CALLED. maid.tech adds the discipline on top: `_Plural.mesa` is the reusable collection panel imported cross-route, `_plural.Thing.mesa` is the folder-scoped part. What it buys is that an import list is readable without paths and a flat search is unambiguous, instead of twelve files named `List.mesa`. **The same app measures how it fails**, which is the substance of the question: a fork keeps the old prefix, so `routes/contacts/` holds four byte-identical copies of `routes/clients/` files still named `_clients.*`, and `routes/messages/` is a fork of `routes/notes/` still named `_notes.*` — at which point the prefix, the one thing that made the convention worth having, is lying. So the convention is worth little stated and a lot checked: *a `_prefix.Part.mesa` whose prefix does not match its folder* is a cheap rule in `core/checks.js`, which already owns everything derived from the file tree, and it catches the exact failure that was measured. Blocked behind [FJS-D114](#fjs-d114) only for the cross-route half — what a `_Plural.mesa` legitimately is depends on whether a Resource is the model's whole client-side surface (§4.6, *a route file may not be imported by a non-route file*) | [IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md](IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md) § 3.4, 4.2 · [sierra README § File roles](packages/sierra/README.md#L186) · [core/checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d38"></a>FJS-D38 | mesa · cli | **Does a TUI reuse `.mesa`, or is it a separate authoring model?** `FJS-D37` ruled that if a TUI is built its engine is bought, and deferred the build past alpha; this is the one part it left open, because the two answers are different projects. Measured 2026-08-17: `runtime.js`'s reactive core (lines 1–760) holds **2** DOM references and the remaining 4,200 lines hold **99**, and the compiler emits a template as an HTML *string* parsed by `htmlToFragment()` then walked by `refer(root, path)` — the dom-expressions strategy, unportable for the same reason it is fast. So **reuse costs a second compiler backend emitting a cell tree, not a renderer**, which is more than it looked like before anyone counted; every Mesa target that exists produces markup, SSR and `email-kit`'s `target: 'email'` included, so nothing in the tree says a non-markup target is cheap. Against that, **separate** means Ink or a wrapper over it — available now, and it makes the framework's answer to *how do I build a terminal app* be *use React*, which is a second component model in a repo whose thesis is one mental model. Nothing is blocked on this today; what it protects is that the tone table and the two output owners `FJS-D37` §1–§2 order do not foreclose either answer | [IDEAS/terminal-surface.md](IDEAS/terminal-surface.md) §5, §9 · [runtime.js](packages/mesa/src/runtime.js) · [compiler.js:1211](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L1211) · `DECISIONS.md` `FJS-D37` |
| <a id="fjs-d25"></a>FJS-D25 | css | **Ship `Lead` and `Kicker` as terms?** Both are concepts the package already names in `tokens.css` and then provides no way to write. `--text-lg` is commented "**lead paragraph**" and `--text-2xs` is "**the uppercase overline**" — the rungs are tuned for these two jobs and neither has a class, so every consumer hand-rolls one. **Lead**: the standfirst under a page title — larger than body, muted, held to a measure. The guide's `.sg-lead` is 18px + `--ink-soft` + 60ch, and two of those three are now Prose's, so the term may be as small as the size. **Kicker**: the short uppercase label ABOVE a title ("Structure", "Learn") — the thing publishing calls a kicker, distinct from a Badge (a status, inline) and from `.divider-label` (a break between things, not a label on one). Evidence it needs a name: the guide drifted **two** of them with no shared owner — `.sg-eyebrow` at 12px/500/accent and `.sg-wiz-eyebrow` at 11px/600/`--ink-mute` — and `.navlist-label` is a third instance of the same shape already shipped but scoped to nav. Three implementations, one idea, no name is the condition that produces exactly that drift. Open questions: does Kicker carry a tone (the guide's is accent-coloured, the wizard's is muted); is Lead just `.text-xl` + Prose's ink, in which case it is a utility composition and not a term; and does a Kicker + title + Lead group want naming as page-header anatomy, or is it a Section with three children (leaning the latter — naming it adds a term that composes entirely from terms). Not to be confused with `.sg-h1`, which is a display treatment (44px/500/serif vs the h1 rung's 36px/700) and is legitimately the guide's own | [packages/css/guide/guide.js](packages/css/guide/guide.js) § pageHeader() · [packages/css/src/foundation/tokens.css](packages/css/src/foundation/tokens.css) § type scale · [packages/css/vocabulary.js](packages/css/vocabulary.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d24"></a>FJS-D24 | css | **Is there a term for a horizontal group that does not wrap — and does Bar mean "a strip" or "a section"?** Two halves of one gap, found putting the guide's topbar onto shipped terms. **First half:** `.cluster` is the horizontal group, and wrapping is its *definition* (`layout.css` § "cluster — horizontal group that wraps"), not a default. Inside anything with a fixed height that is wrong — a Topbar has `--topbar-height`, so a wrapped second line is drawn OUTSIDE the bar rather than growing it. Measured at 640px: the guide's version badge landed at y=46 in a 56px bar starting at y=8, on top of the page below. Nothing in the vocabulary says *row, together, one line*: Split forces `space-between`, Toolbar promises arrow-key movement the app must implement, and Bar is the right geometry with the wrong scale. Candidate is a `.cluster.nowrap` modifier — one line, `NOT_A_TERM` as a modifier, and it generalises to every fixed-height container. **Second half:** the guide now writes `bar start` for the two groups inside its Topbar, which works and reads oddly — Bar is Region-tier and means *a horizontal strip*, so two of them inside a Topbar says there are three strips where there is one. The missing concept may be a Bar-shaped thing at a smaller scale (a *group* within a strip) rather than a Cluster modifier; if that is the answer it subsumes the first half. Deliberately unruled — the guide's use is marked in place | [packages/css/guide/guide.js](packages/css/guide/guide.js) § topbar() · [packages/css/src/foundation/layout.css](packages/css/src/foundation/layout.css) · [packages/css/src/patterns/bars.css](packages/css/src/patterns/bars.css) |

## Closed

Kept so a stale document cannot reopen them. Newest first.

**Older closures live in [ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md).** This
section holds the recent ones and is trimmed past ~40 rows; the archive holds the
rest, verbatim. An id is never reused, so a `FJS-###` named anywhere in the repo
resolves in one file or the other.

| Id | Title | Closed | How |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| <a id="fjs-432"></a>FJS-432 | basecamp — **`AuditEvent` was gated and unpolicied, so the trail was cross-workspace at the Data boundary** | 2026-08-25 | Ruled as [FJS-D141](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d141) and the ruling is most of the fix: the framework had already answered this in [FJS-D05](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d05) — *on a read a row holding no tenant belongs to nobody and stays invisible* — so the model needed a DELETED line rather than a hand-written policy. `@@tenant(none)` gone, the declared `tenancy { }` applies, and the trail is scoped where `db/access.snapshot.md` can show it instead of in one service's `where`. **The row asked whether null means *global*, and the database answered**: 19 null-workspace rows in the dev database, of which 6 were hub actions and **12 were `jobs.startRun`/`finishRun` losing their stamp** — `internalOnly()` methods are exempt from `sessionScope`, so `ctx.locals.workspaceId` was absent and a workspace's own `Job` runs filed under nobody, invisible to the only feed that wants them. Fixed first and separately, because sealing the meaning in over a mis-stamped column hides those rows permanently behind a correct-looking policy: the audit hook already re-reads the subject row for its diff, so the subject's own `workspaceId` answers second and a `Workspace` is its own workspace. Three tests, each red without its half. **It also found a defect in litestone's own harness** — the factory does not skip an `auth()` default, which is right on a REQUIRED foreign key (`_freshParents` overwrites it with a real parent) and wrong on a nullable one, where nothing seeds a parent and the generated string reached SQLite as `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed` at every level of the ladder. A fixture never invents a foreign key now. The two spellings of *null tenant* still disagree and that is [FJS-528](#fjs-528) | [schema.lite](packages/basecamp/db/schema.lite) · [hooks.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts) · [testing.js](packages/litestone/src/testing.js) |
| <a id="fjs-385"></a>FJS-385 | **A tenant can differ in its DATA and in nothing else — there is no per-tenant configuration** | 2026-08-25 | Ruled and built as [FJS-D126](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d126). `createApp({ tenantConfig, tenantConfigKeys })` — a resolver answering a plain object per tenant over `app.config` as the floor, read through `$.config` / `app.configFor(tenant)`. The Orthicon shape the row cites is the one it serves, and the row's own worry — *a config an app reads through `app.config` is resolved before any request exists* — is what the three-phase order answered: the READ moved to call scope first, under no feature and at no behavioural cost, and the source filled the interior afterwards. The resolve happens in the around hook that already establishes the tenant, because a property read cannot await. `tenantConfigKeys` is the safe half and is committed to `principal.snapshot.md` |
| <a id="fjs-d126"></a>FJS-D126 | **Does a TENANT carry configuration, and where does it resolve?** | 2026-08-25 | **Ruled: yes, in three clauses.** The READ is `$.config` / `app.configFor(tenant)` through one owner, shipped ahead of the source because the same boundary move under a live feature means finding every reader again. The view is **read-only, deep**, and `app.config` is never written — the clause the prior art decides, since Laravel, Django and NestJS each rebind or forbid and each pays for it, while junction has the ALS already. The SOURCE is `createApp({ tenantConfig })`, a resolver rather than a declaration on [FJS-D113](#fjs-d113)'s ground, memoised per tenant (the promise, not the value) with `invalidateTenantConfig` as the explicit way out and a failed resolve deliberately not memoised. **The resolve happens where the tenant is already resolved** — the around hook that sets `ctx.locals.tenantId`, and `runAs` — because a property read cannot await; a resolver that throws fails the call rather than quietly serving the floor. **`tenantConfigKeys` is required and `RESERVED_CONFIG_PATHS` is refused at boot** (`port`, `host`, `database`, `http`, `auth`, `apiPrefix`, `logging`): a database path handed to a tenant is every other tenant's rows, and the rest are read at boot with no tenant in scope. A key the resolver answers that the list does not name is refused by name, never dropped. Laravel's `storage_to_config_map` is the shape; what is added is that **the list is committed**, as § Per-tenant configuration in `principal.snapshot.md` ([FJS-514](#fjs-514)) — the half that makes the feature safe is the half worth diffing. 31 tests | [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d126) § API design · [FJS-385](#fjs-385) · [IDEAS/tenancy-exterior.md](IDEAS/tenancy-exterior.md) §6 |
| <a id="fjs-514"></a>FJS-514 | **The tenancy predicate is committed and its input is not, so a resolver emitting the WRONG tenant leaves every artefact byte-identical** | 2026-08-25 | `junction principal --app <module>` writes `principal.snapshot.md` at the app root, read off a BUILT app because a resolver is wired in application code and no file tree can answer which one an app ended up with. It names its own generator in its header, so the `snapshots` phase reruns it with no CI edit — verified by tampering with a row and watching the phase fail with the regeneration command. Seven sections: tenancy · resolver · claims · standing · what the rules reach · the three refusals · **per-tenant configuration, empty until [FJS-D126](#fjs-d126) is ruled**, which exists before the feature so *which keys a tenant may override* lands as a diff rather than a new face. `membershipClaim()` describes itself and the return type says so; `tenantFrom` is excluded for being a function and `namedBy` included for being a constant — **names, never values**. The value→level mapping is deliberately absent and named as such: `getLevel` is a function handed to `GatePlugin` and a client answers plugin names rather than instances, so the enum values are committed and the mapping stays `verifyGateLadder`'s. **Two things it got wrong before being run against a real app**, both saying something false rather than nothing: it asked `app.db` for the declaration, which under `createApp({ tenants })` does not exist, and reported *no tenancy* about an app that is nothing but; and it rendered a `bearer` resolver as a membership one with five blank cells and its claim as a standing. Committed for `example` (`strategy database`, bearer) and `packages/basecamp` (`strategy row`, membership — 17 scoped, 14 delegated, 14 exempt, the same split [FJS-513](#fjs-513) reaches independently). 11 tests, junction typecheck clean at its 0 baseline |
| <a id="fjs-513"></a>FJS-513 | **`verifyRowPolicies` reports a delegated model as `skipped`, so the deepest half of declared tenancy has no executed proof** | 2026-08-25 | `verifyTenantIsolation()`, the fifth executed check. It does not teach the grader to evaluate a `check()` — that is the oracle problem, two implementations of one rule grading each other — it **executes the crossing**: seed a row for tenant A, then have tenant B and a caller holding no claim try to reach it, on five moves. `read`/`update`/`delete` are B reaching into A; `create` is B placing a row inside A's tenant and `post-update` is B moving a row it owns into it, which are the two the generated `post-update` deny exists for and the two a column comparison does not cover. **Both sides asserted** — A must reach its own row or the result is `unreachable`, since a policy admitting nobody and a model nothing was seeded into are one observation — and **every model is named**, because a model that isolates correctly is silent and so is a model nothing ran against. Three answers that are not verdicts: `unscoped` (no column, no scoped parent, no exemption — the shape the desugar says nothing about), `unparented` ([FJS-382](#fjs-382)'s ruling, where an optional scoping relation lets a row belong to no tenant) and `exempt`. `strategy database` is reported by name: one client cannot hold two files. **Two defects in the build itself, both found by running it**: the seeder skipped optional scoped relations, so a delegated model was graded only in its degenerate unparented form; and the per-model restore rolled back past the two tenants, so the first model that passed took the fixture with it and the other 28 failed to seed. `_tenantStamp` is now shared with `verifyFieldProtection` rather than copied ([FJS-381](#fjs-381)). **basecamp: 31 graded (17 column, 14 delegated), 14 exempt, 18 uncheckable, no leak.** The fourteen had never been graded by anything. litestone 3022 pass, 0 fail. **Run by basecamp's own suite** (`db/test/schema.test.ts`, beside the other four) rather than by a one-off script — a checker nothing runs is the defect one level up, and the first close of this row had exactly that shape. The suite asserts COVERAGE as a set rather than a count, so it survives the schema growing and still fails a model that quietly drops out; proved to fire by appending an unscoped model to a copy of basecamp's real schema, which reports `unscoped` by name |
| <a id="fjs-515"></a>FJS-515 | toolbelt — **`glow` could not highlight three of the four languages this repo writes, and could not highlight a transcript at all** | 2026-08-25 | Found by moving `website/` off its own hand-rolled highlighter and onto the framework's. **`.lite`** went through the generic pass, where both halves of a schema came out wrong: the attribute rule can take one `@`, so it matched at the SECOND one and every `@@gate` in every schema rendered as a stray punctuation mark followed by an attribute; and a field's type was coloured only where the common keyword list happened to contain it case-insensitively, so `Int` and `String` were lit while `DateTime`, `Json` and a relation to another model were not — the column that says what a field IS, half-lit down the page. **SQL** had nothing at all: no keyword in the common list is SQL and `--` was not read as a comment, so a `CREATE TABLE` was one unlit line with its string literals coloured. **Shell** had the opposite problem — the common list contains `my`, `use`, `end`, `local`, `next`, `get` and `set`, all of them ordinary argument text, so `cd my-app` came out with `my` as a keyword and `-app` as punctuation after it: the directory name a reader is meant to type, in three pieces, on an install command. Shell is in the keyword-exclusion list now beside yaml/html/json, and what replaces it is the COMMAND word, which is the token a reader is looking for. **And a `language` may now be a LIST.** A transcript — a command and its output, a query beside the WHERE clause a policy appended to it — is one block and two languages, and given either alone half of it goes dark: under `js` a `--` comment is two punctuation marks, under `sql` a `//` comment is not a comment. The first entry stays primary and is what the wrapper's `language` attribute carries, so `@frontierjs/css`'s theme is unaffected. `mesa` was registered as a mixed-HTML language while there. Five tests, each asserting the text is unchanged as well as the marking. Green: toolbelt 210, css 470 | [glow.js](packages/toolbelt/src/glow/glow.js) · [glow.spec.js](packages/toolbelt/test/specs/glow.spec.js) · [code.css](packages/css/src/components/code.css) |
| <a id="fjs-510"></a>FJS-510 | litestone — **a field `@allow` on a relation field parsed and enforced nothing, in both directions** | 2026-08-25 | `team Team @relation(fields: [teamId]) @allow('write', …)` was accepted and a caller the predicate refused moved the row to another team anyway; `@allow('read', …)` on the same field returned the relation to everyone. A relation is not stored, so the write half had no `col` for its `CASE WHEN` and the read half stripped a key the row never carried. **Refused at parse now, naming the column that works** — `@relation(fields: [teamId])` already says which one — and separately for an implicit many-to-many, which has no column on this model at all. The FK spelling was measured first and guards **both doors**: the direct `teamId` write and the `{ team: { connect: { id } } }` form. A `@derived` field is deliberately left alone: it rides the SELECT as an expression, so the read strip does reach it — verified, rather than assumed from `isStoredField`, which would have refused it wrongly. Two tests, and the existing *non-enum field* case was re-pinned to the new sentence. Green: litestone 3013 |
| <a id="fjs-511"></a>FJS-511 | litestone — **`@@transitions` could not name a Boolean column** | 2026-08-25 | `@@transitions(isPrimary, promote: false -> true, demote: true -> false @gate(5))` died in the tokeniser — *Expected IDENT, got 'false'* — so the two-state machine every schema has (`isPrimary`, `isPublished`, `isSuspended`) had no declaration, and its two directions, which are routinely different authorities, could only be guarded by one field `@allow` answering both with one predicate. The name-only version was written first and MEASURED — it reports eleven of basecamp's nineteen moves and is wrong about eight. Asking for either reports three, all real (`FJS-517`), and `example` clean. **The two spellings are not symmetrical**, which writing a test the wrong way round is what found: `transition()` resolves a move NAME, so the target-state spelling works for `update()` and throws here — unless the move is unnamed, since `pending -> paid` names itself after its target. The parse costs two things worth stating: the clause list splits on TOP-LEVEL commas only (a from-list is itself comma-separated, and splitting naively loses the name and reports the target), and the name is optional. | <a id="fjs-495"></a>FJS-495 | litestone · sierra — **`transitions(row)` graded the gate and not the policies, so a UI drew a button the Data boundary refused** | 2026-08-25 | A move is an update, so an `@@allow('update', …)` refuses one exactly as a gate does — and `allowed` was `gate == null ? true : level >= gate`, consulting no policy at all. That is the answer to the cost half the row raised: a machine's moves share few targets, and a model with no policies evaluates nothing. **`refusedBy` is new on every entry** (`'gate'` · `'policy'` · `null`), because a screen renders *you are not senior enough* and *not this record* differently and nothing else separates them — the gate is null for every ungated move a policy refuses. **`policyVerdict` is the one answer to *does this policy admit this row*** — `checkCreatePolicy` and `checkPostUpdatePolicy` were one function under two op names and this is a third caller that must NOT throw. Deny-before-allow, an empty `allows` meaning *no opinion* rather than *nobody*, and `asSystem()` skipping outright are three rules a second copy would get wrong. It deliberately does not catch: at the boundary an undecidable policy must refuse, and the affordance is where permissiveness belongs. **Sierra's mirror is unchanged and now says why** — `x-transitions` carries a gate and not a predicate, a browser has no policy engine, so that half answers `allowed: true` for a policy-refused move; its docblock claimed *mirrors litestone's `transitions(row)` field for field*, which had stopped being true. On a static target `{}` means no `render: static`, so the route is not prerendered; it does not reach `skipped` either, because it never claimed to be static. | <a id="fjs-500"></a>FJS-500 | mesa · sierra — **a prerendered page did not escape its own text, so any string a static build baked in was live markup** | 2026-08-25 | `<p>{text}</p>` sets `textContent` at runtime and is the safest expression in the language; at build time it renders into happy-dom and serialises with `container.innerHTML`, and **happy-dom 14.12.3 did not re-escape a text node on the way out**. Nothing in Mesa's own code was wrong, which is exactly why nothing here could see it: the client path is correct and only the round trip through the DOM was not, so the runtime suite and the browser drives all agreed the escaping worked. Measured through the real path — `renderComponent` over `<p>{text}</p>` with `text = '<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>'` emitted `<p><img src=x onerror=alert(1)></p>` — and at the bare serialiser, where `div.textContent = '<script>alert(1)<\/script>'` reads back as live markup. Every `render: static` route baking database strings was affected, `example/site` included, where a product name, description, brand and image `alt` all reach the file this way; a static page is served to anyone, cached by a CDN and cannot be recalled. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by the bump `FJS-475` was already asking for — `^14.12.3` → `^20.11.6`, which escapes text nodes, preserves an entity written in the source and does not double-escape an ampersand. **Nine tests in `render-ssr.test.js` pin the behaviour and were confirmed to FAIL on 14.12.3 before the bump was restored**. Five of them are the ATTRIBUTE half, which this report did not name and which is the wider hole: a text node can only inject an element, and an attribute value that escapes its own quotes injects an EVENT HANDLER onto an element the page already trusts — measured through the same round trip, `setAttribute('title', '" onmouseover=alert(1) autofocus x="')` emitted `<a href="/x" title="" onmouseover=alert(1) autofocus x="">`, which re-parses to a link carrying `onmouseover`, `autofocus`, `x` and a stray `1`. `<img alt={…}>` and `<a href={…}>` are database strings on every prerendered catalogue page. They assert by RE-PARSING the output and counting the element's attributes rather than by matching text, because `title="&quot; onmouseover=alert(1)"` contains the characters `onmouseover=` and is completely safe: the question is whether an attribute was ADDED, not how a value was spelled. The payload is asserted to survive intact as a value, since escaping is not sanitising and a product called `5" pipe` must come back as `5" pipe`, because this is an assertion about a dependency and a version range is not a test: a downgrade, a second resolved copy or a serialiser regression is now red rather than an injected script on a public page. Green: mesa 1300 + 82 + 47, sierra 1109, email-kit 34, ui 857. | <a id="fjs-460"></a>FJS-460 | junction — **`app.stop()` waited the whole drain timeout and then reported success with the client's socket still open** | 2026-08-26 | `stop()` raced Bun's graceful `server.stop()` against `drainTimeout` and closed nothing. **Bun's graceful stop never resolves once a WebSocket has been upgraded** — measured against a bare `Bun.serve`, not inferred: not after the socket is closed, not after the server's own close handler has run and the client reads `readyState 3`. So the race always ended on the timer: every shutdown that had ever held a client cost the full five seconds, and then logged *Shutdown complete* with that client still at `readyState 1`, told nothing. A suite whose `afterAll` is that call outlived bun's 5s hook limit **four runs in five**, which is how it arrived — `tests/query-parity.test.ts` was the first test in the repo to open a socket it did not close. An app meets the same thing as a container that will not stop and is SIGKILLed. **The waits are junction's own now and neither is Bun's promise**: the transport keeps the set of sockets it has open — at the TRANSPORT, because a socket belongs to it and not to the channels plugin, so an app's own `app.http.ws(...)` route is covered too — says goodbye to each, waits for the close handshakes and any in-flight request bounded by `drainTimeout`, then stops by force. 5002ms → **2ms**, with the client reading a clean close. **The close code is 1012 Service Restart and not 1001 Going Away**, which is the code that means this: sent as 1001 it reaches the peer as **1000**, while 1012 and a private 4001 arrive unchanged — so the one code for *the server is going away* is the one Bun does not deliver. Pinned, so a Bun release that fixes it says so. One claim in the report did not survive contact: the port was released promptly even before, because a graceful stop stops ACCEPTING at once and only its promise hangs. 7 tests in `tests/shutdown.test.ts`, timings included, since a test that only asserted `stop()` resolves passed before the fix as well. Green: junction 1418 (`FJS-516` is the one red and predates this) |
| <a id="fjs-505"></a>FJS-505 | mesa — **a `$:` watch on a value with no depth switched that variable's own reactivity off, silently** | 2026-08-25 | The bare `$: a` form is the DEEP-watch opt-in — it is what changes `a`'s accessor from `$$runtime.get($$sig_a)` to `$$proxy_a`, so a mutation three levels down notifies, and the compiler's own comment says so. On a PRIMITIVE there is nothing to proxy: `localWatchProxy` hands the value straight back, `$$proxy_a` is an ordinary local holding a snapshot, and **every read of `a` in the component compiles to a plain variable read**, so the render effect subscribes to nothing. Value right, screen stale, and no error, no warning and no console output anywhere. Measured against the compiler by toggling one line — `var __a = $$proxy_n > 2` with the watch, `var __a = $$runtime.get($$sig_n) > 2` without — and narrowed to exactly one form: `$: a, () => {}` (a watch with a body) and `$: d = a` (a derivation) both emit the signal read and were never affected, which is why `@frontierjs/ui`'s Combobox and DatePicker work. **Refused rather than repaired, and the reason is that the declaration is redundant** — a local `let` is already a signal, so a whole-value watch on one can only add deep-mutation tracking and a primitive has no depth. Nothing is lost by refusing. The tracking fix was the alternative and is not free: it would make `ctx.accessors[root]` a sequence expression, and two call sites compare that string by equality. Conservative on purpose — only an initialiser that is VISIBLY a primitive refuses (`0`, `''`, `false`, `null`, a template literal, no initialiser at all), because `let x = count()` has the same hole and is not decidable at compile time, and a rule that guessed would refuse `let rows = []`. **A sweep of all 313 components in the repo found one other instance, and it was live**: `$: (handoffError)` in `example`'s basket screen compiled the alert's `{#if}` to `() => ($$proxy_handoffError) ? 0 : null`, so a spent or expired checkout link showed a shopper nothing at all — the API refusal had a drive and the screen had none. `verify:widget` covers it now (37), and the assertion needed a real navigation: the browser was already on `/cart/`, and changing only the FRAGMENT reloads no document. Found building the discount box, where `let discountInput = ''` with a watch beside it left the Apply button permanently disabled while the box filled underneath it. 19 tests in `test/watch-no-depth.test.js` — the refusal matrix both ways, the message naming the variable and both ways out, and three behavioural cases mounting real components: a plain `let` re-rendering on its own, and the deep watch still tracking a mutation on an object and on a named path. So adding a service and regenerating the snapshot — which CI forces — updates the dev proxy and the deploy's Caddy config together, and there is nothing left to remember. **It was stale a sixth time when this was written**: `/connections` was mounted and absent. That is the half no author can be expected to hold — it is junction's own channels plugin, as `/account` and `/sessions` are auth's and `/conduit-targets` is conduit's, so three of the six stalenesses were paths this app's source does not name anywhere. **The parse is graded against the running app, not against the file it read**, because a change to junction's output shape would otherwise leave it finding fewer paths and saying nothing — the same silence one layer along. The test walks `app.services.list()` and `buildRoutes(app)` and asserts every mounted service and every raw route is proxied, that `/` (the shell, mounted by `staticRoutes`) is not, and that `/ws` is stated rather than derived, since the channels plugin upgrades in the transport and appears in no router. **The durable fix is untouched and now free**: with an `apiPrefix` the derivation answers that one path and retires itself, asserted so adopting it stays a config change. It broke the image on the way past and that is the measured half — the Dockerfile copies `api db web tsconfig*` and not the snapshot, while `bun run build:web` runs INSIDE the image and loads `vite.config.js`, so the SPA build stopped before a line of it ran; one `COPY surface.snapshot.md ./` and the image builds. Proven through a real dev proxy rather than by reading the config: `/connections`, `/invitations` and `/projects` asked for as JSON reach the API (401 — it arrived), the same URLs asked for as a document get the shell, and a path the API does not mount falls to Vite's own 404. Green: 151 tests, `fli check` 21 rules, typecheck at baseline, `docker build` exit 0 | [api-paths.js](packages/basecamp/web/config/api-paths.js) · [surface.snapshot.md](packages/basecamp/surface.snapshot.md) · [Dockerfile](packages/basecamp/deploy/Dockerfile) |
| <a id="fjs-384"></a>FJS-384 | basecamp — **Five job handlers opened `asSystem()` behind a comment saying a job has no caller to scope to** | 2026-08-25 | **The framework half shipped 2026-08-23** — caravan records `actor_id` and `tenant_id` at dispatch, junction re-binds both through `app.runAs`, and `membershipClaim` re-reads the membership when the job runs, so an actor who lost access between asking and running is refused rather than replayed. The app never adopted it: the comment stopped being true and nothing changed. **What adoption turned out to mean is not what this row assumed.** Measured against the app's own schema rather than reasoned about: every row these handlers write is gated at SYSTEM for update — `RecipeRun`, `DeploymentStep`, `CleanupRun` at `@@gate("2.4.8.8")`, `JobRun` at `2.8` — and the highest standing a workspace grants is `owner`, 6. That is the schema saying a run's OUTCOME belongs to the machine and not to whoever asked, and a gate refuses regardless of standing, so no amount of tenant-binding reaches those rows. `asSystem()` there is the declared design. **What was actually missing is CONFINEMENT**, and that is what shipped: each handler's reads and writes go through an `internalOnly()` method on the service that owns the row, which reads the PARENT through the caller's own client first — a release, run or job in another workspace answers nothing, where an id off a payload used to be written wherever it pointed — and only then does the gated write as system. The audit actor and the announcement come back with it, which `asSystem()` had been dropping; `deployment:run` had also been pushing the row by hand and `job:run` had not been pushing at all. **The rule is dispatch declares, handler asserts** (`api/src/jobs/context.ts`): `runsAsCaller` refuses unless the queue recorded an actor AND a tenant, `runsAsApp` refuses when it recorded one, and `runsEitherWay` is `job:run`, the one job dispatched both by a person and by a cron. The cron fire in `job-schedule.ts` now STATES `actor: null` — caravan would have defaulted to nobody there anyway, and saying it is what makes the handler's refusal mean something. `job:run` keeps ONE write path for both modes: the confinement read is scoped when there is a caller and system when the mode says there is not, since a cron fire is the app acting on its own behalf across workspaces. Nine methods added across four services, all `internalOnly` and all in `surface.snapshot.md` — junction answers 405 to a name `methods:` leaves out, in-process included, so an engine method has to be declared surface and the hook is what keeps it off the wire. **5 tests**, including the one that matters: an `owner` of one workspace cannot open a run in another, the owner of THAT workspace can, and the same principal moved to a wire transport is refused by the hook rather than by the session. Green: basecamp 35 API-tier tests, typecheck 14 (one under baseline), 7 snapshots current | [jobs/context.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/jobs/context.ts) · [hooks.ts § internalOnly](packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts) · [job-schedule.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/services/jobs/job-schedule.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-279"></a>FJS-279 | jetty — **nothing in jetty could talk to a real Junction** | 2026-08-25 | `createJunctionAdapter` (`@frontierjs/jetty/junction`), over `@frontierjs/junction/client` — the browser client every Sierra app already uses, rather than a second implementation written to the same protocol. The subscription question the issue left open answers itself: there is nothing for a client to send, because membership is the SERVER's (`app.channel(name).join(…)`), and what arrives is `{ type: 'event', event: 'orders pay' }` re-emitted as `client.on('event', …)` — so a channel is the first half of that name and a subscription is a filter over it. Two things had to move for it to be honest. **`url` is spelled differently by the two packages** — jetty's config field has always been `wss://` and the client takes an http origin and derives the socket, so `wsss://` and a socket that never opens; both are accepted. And **sign-in is `adapter.auth`, not `call('auth', …)`**: Junction has no service by that name — auth registers `account`, `sessions` and `api-keys`, and establishing a session is a route (`FJS-D20`) — so the placeholder's pseudo-service would shadow the methods of any app that has one. `makeAuthFlow` prefers the block and falls back to the call. | <a id="fjs-280"></a>FJS-280 | repo — **neither optional surface had an instance in a real app** | 2026-08-25 | Both halves are `example`'s now. `widgets/` came first (`verify:widget` — a buy button on a page the shop does not own, four origins, a real preflight). `extension/` is this one: `example/extension/`, a harbor holding the only connection, a Mesa dock in the popup, and a content script on the shop's own prerendered storefront — `verify:extension`, 13 assertions. | <a id="fjs-492"></a>FJS-492 | litestone — **a tenant registry dropped `clientOptions.databases`, so the one path a fleet does not own could not be pinned** | 2026-08-25 | Found by an orphan `example/site/db/shop.db`, created two days earlier and invisible because the repo ignores `*.db*` everywhere. | <a id="fjs-410"></a>FJS-410 | basecamp — **A workspace admin could promote themselves to owner, so level 5 granted itself level 6** | 2026-08-24 | Found by `gate-over-own-standing` in litestone's `advise.js`, which can SEE that `WorkspaceMember` is where the tenancy claim is read from and therefore that its `role` column is the one every gate in the app is graded off. **Closed in three tiers, and the last one is the door a person actually walks through.** The Data boundary came first: `@@deny('update', userId == auth().id)`, which covers a job, a hub screen and a `fli tinker` session at level 5 — and the first attempt at it was a FIELD policy, `role @allow('write', userId != auth().id)`, which reads like the rule and is none, because a field write predicate is graded against the PAYLOAD, so `userId` is undefined in a patch of `{ role }` and the column is permitted (`FJS-433`). Then the read half, since a model with no `@@allow` for an operation is OPEN and a scoped client was reading every workspace's membership — four allows, read being `userId == auth().id` so the switcher still answers across workspaces, which is why `@@tenant(none)` is on this model. **Then the service tier, 2026-08-24, and the row had called that half a missing sentence — it was not.** Driven through `@frontierjs/testing` against the real app: an admin naming their own `userId` with `role: 'owner'` got **no throw, a 200, and OWNER(6)**. No schema rule can reach that path — `members()` is `asSystem()`, because membership is what decides access and cannot be read through the caller it is deciding about, and `asSystem()` is the context every policy is bypassed in. Three refusals, because the escalation has three shapes and *not your own row* is only the first: a self-check in `setMemberRole` (your own role, in either direction); `refuseRoleAboveOwn()`, registered on `workspaces.addMember`, `workspaces.setMemberRole` and `invitations.create`, because an admin who cannot promote themselves can invite an address they own AS owner and sign in as it; and an outrank check, because `Cannot demote the last owner` only catches an admin demoting an owner where there happens to be exactly one. Equal is allowed — an admin appointing an admin is what the role is for. The hook is registered per method rather than on `all`: `role` is a word two other models use, and a hook grading every payload carrying the key would refuse a fleet write for holding the wrong kind of role. **6 tests in `api/test/services.test.ts`, mutation-checked** — neuter the hook and two go red, the self-check one, the outrank check one. The members screen narrows both pickers to what the caller may hand out (a slice of the enum, which is declared in ascending authority, rather than a fourth copy of the ladder) and disables the row that is your own; an affordance, with the refusal in the service. Both tables are swept by the one cleanup timer. **A test already existed for the defect and asserted the accident**: `tokens do not cross protocols` expected `UserNotFoundError`, which is what the door being OPEN looks like from outside — the token did cross, and what refused it was an address lookup missing on a prefix two steps later. | <a id="fjs-474"></a>FJS-474 | sierra — **The boot restore short-circuits on `!client.token`, so a `cookieAuth` app renders signed out on every cold load while a valid session sits in the jar** | 2026-08-24 | `initSession` read `client.token`, which is the BEARER answer to a question that has two modes. The fix names the question: `client.hasCredential` on the browser client — `cookieAuth || !!token` — where false means *there is definitely no credential*, the only safe reason to skip asking, and true means *ask*, which is not a claim that the session is valid. `cookieAuth` joins `apiPrefix`, `authPrefix` and `authServices` as a client option that MUST match the server's; the client cannot read the server's plugin options and these are two programs. **A second half was found while fixing it and is closed here too**: the boot socket branch read `client.token` for the same reason, so a cookie-mode app opened no WebSocket after any reload, for the life of the page, with nothing saying so. It waits on the session restore rather than on `hasCredential`, because that is true for a stranger too and opening a socket for every anonymous visitor to a public page is a cost the Bearer path never pays. **The stub was the reason it survived a suite of 1088**: `tests/session.test.js` builds a fake client, so the contract it models was the contract that was wrong — the repo's own *fake clients hide real bugs* rule, in the one place nobody applies it. | <a id="fjs-473"></a>FJS-473 | sierra — **the scanner plugin took the BUILD branch on every dev boot, crashed on its own warn path, and could not fail a build with the error it raises** | 2026-08-23 | Three defects in one function, found by `bun run dev:site` refusing to start with `ReferenceError: warn is not defined`. **(1)** `buildStart` read `this.environment?.mode !== 'serve'` to decide whether it was a build. It reads `command` off `configResolved` now, which is `serve` or `build` and is the question being asked. **(2)** `checkStaticPaths` referenced `warn`, which is a parameter of `runScan` and not of it — so the one branch that mentions a companion it could not import threw a `ReferenceError` instead of warning. Only the import is guarded now. The warning carries `err.message` now; without it the message read as the companion being absent when it is usually the companion's own imports throwing. The pattern was `/-[A-Za-z0-9_]{8,}\.[a-z0-9]+$/`: the hash is base64url and may contain a `-`, and one that does was read as an unhashed name. | <a id="fjs-472"></a>FJS-472 | junction — **the webhooks plugin signed `${timestamp}.${body}`, so a captured signature replayed against any other endpoint on the same receiver** | 2026-08-23 | Filed and closed in one pass, because it was found by reading `@frontierjs/toolbelt/signature`'s own header, which names this delivery path as one of the three signers it exists to unify — and the drift was not cosmetic. The old string bound neither the METHOD nor the PATH, so a subscriber trusting one secret across two endpoints could have a signature moved between them, and it carried no NONCE, so a repeat inside the freshness window was indistinguishable from a first delivery. | <a id="fjs-442"></a>FJS-442 | litestone — **a field-level `@allow('read', …)` stripped the column and left it fully filterable and sortable, so its value came back by binary search** | 2026-08-23 | Measured: `salary Int? @allow('read', auth().isAdmin)`, written through `asSystem()`, read back by a non-admin as absent — the strip worked — and recovered exactly as `91000` in seventeen requests by `where: { salary: { gt: mid } }`. `orderBy` leaks the ordering of every row in one. **`FJS-393`'s answer does not extend to it**, which is why the row was filed apart: `@guarded` is a set-membership test decided once per model, and this is a predicate — refusing every filter on a predicated column would also refuse the one a caller may legitimately run over the rows they CAN read it on. **Ruled and built as [FJS-D129](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d129)**, with `FJS-433`: the predicate is compiled by the same `compileSql` row policies use and AND-ed into the caller's arguments as a **SIBLING** of their `where`. A row-dependent predicate therefore still narrows to the caller's own rows rather than refusing. **The cost is stated rather than discovered**: an `OR` branch naming the column narrows the whole read, including branches that do not name it — safe (fewer rows, never more), blunt, and two reads is the way out. Precision there is what the rejected alternative, masking the column with a `CASE` at its reference, would have bought; it was rejected because a masked expression carries params and a single clause repeats the column an unbounded number of times. ELSE col END`, so it reads the STORED row — a caller cannot assert past it, and a bulk update grades every row separately, which no JS evaluation of one payload can do. | <a id="fjs-388"></a>FJS-388 | cli · litestone — **the development workflow wrote tables and the deploy workflow applied files, and the two never met** | 2026-08-23 | Every generator told a developer to run `fli db:push`, which diffs the schema against the live database and creates tables directly, writing no migration file. The container entrypoint is `bun run db:migrate && bun run start`, and that is `litestone migrate apply`, which applies migration FILES. A model added through push was in the developer's database and in no file, so the next image was missing it: `migrate apply` printed `no migration files found` with a TICK, exited **0**, `/health` answered 200, the deploy was declared good, and `POST /auth/register` answered 500 `no such table: user`. A table-granular guard on 2026-08-22 was reported as most of the fix and **was not**: re-measured 2026-08-23, it compares declared table NAMES to `sqlite_master`, so a new *model* is caught and a new **column** is not — history at `User{id,email}`, a pushed `name` column, `✓ 1 migration applied`, exit 0, over a table with no `name`. A column add is the common change after week one, so the guard passed exactly the traffic it was written for. Worse, **the advice it printed was a closed loop**: *write one: `litestone migrate create`*, and that command answered `already in sync — no migration needed`, because `create()` diffed the schema against the LIVE database, which a pushed database already matches. No way out from inside the tool. **Ruled and fixed as [FJS-D123](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d123)**: one comparison was doing two jobs, so there is now a SHADOW — the migration files replayed into memory — and two questions: *schema ↔ shadow* (what migration is missing) and *shadow ↔ live* (has somebody changed the database). `migrate create` diffs against the shadow, so the delta a push hid is written; the deploy guard is schema-granular over `diffSchemas`, so a column is caught; `migrate baseline` records files as applied without running them, refusing when the database does not actually hold what they build; `migrate dev` is create + apply + drift-check, which is what makes `db push` prototyping-only rather than a workflow a deployed app lives in; and `migrate check` asks the deploy's question of the REPO alone — no database, no container, no network — so `fli deploy:doctor` asks it before the image is built and `migrate apply` asks the same function again at container start. `fli check` carries the structural half. Prisma's shadow database and `migrate resolve --applied` are the prior art, and ZenStack v3 is the sharper precedent: it rewrote Prisma's ORM on Kysely and did **not** rewrite its migration engine. 19 cases in `test/migration-history.test.ts`, 4 in the cli's `checks.test.js`. | <a id="fjs-345"></a>FJS-345 | cli · repo — **a scaffolded app built, deployed, answered health and had NO TABLES, so the first real request was a 500** | 2026-08-23 | `fli new` wrote `db/schema.lite` and never a `db/migrations/`, while the image's entrypoint is `bun run db:migrate && bun run start` — a script that APPLIES existing migrations and creates none. The container logged `no migration files found`, started clean, passed `/api/health`, and answered `POST /api/auth/register` with `500 no such table: user`. Both package sources, so the pipeline rather than a package. Precisely the class the `deploy` CI phase exists to catch ([FJS-252](#fjs-252)) — health says the process is up, not that the app works. The scaffold half closed first: `fli new` writes the initial migration with `migrate create`, which needs no database. **The general case is closed by [FJS-D123](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d123)** with [FJS-388](#fjs-388), because the scaffold fix only ever covered the FIRST occurrence — a model added afterwards through `fli db:push` was missing from the next image again. `litestone migrate check` now answers *does the history build the schema* from the repo alone, `fli deploy:doctor` asks it before the image is built, `migrate apply` refuses at container start, and `fli check` carries the structural half — an app whose entrypoint replays migrations must have some to replay. **Found TWICE more while wiring that check**, which is the argument for having it: `example`, this repo's own kitchen-sink app, had no `db/migrations/` at all and could not have been deployed — its initial migration is now committed; and CI's own `scaffold` phase built an app in the same state, because it scaffolds with `--no-install` and `fli new` can only write the initial migration when it installed (`migrate create` is `bunx litestone`). The phase installs afterwards and never ran the step, so the app it graded green every run was one that could not deploy. It now writes the migration after the install, which is what `fli new`'s own warning tells a user who declined the install to do | [project/new.md](packages/cli/commands/project/new.md) · [checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) `migration-history` · [FJS-D123](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d123) |

| <a id="fjs-428"></a>FJS-428 | junction — **an app could not send a caller-varied header over the socket at all, and the one value that needed it was hardcoded on both sides** | 2026-08-23 | Over HTTP a value the caller varies per call is a header. | <a id="fjs-412"></a>FJS-412 | litestone · sierra · ui — **value sets: a name for a scoped list, enforced at the Data boundary and rendered from it** | 2026-08-22 | `FJS-D120` ruled the shape and this is the whole of it. `valueset <Name> { source · value · scope · where }` plus `@values(<Name>[, required|open|suggested])`, thirteen refusal shapes at parse, `x-values` on the field schema, and one branch each in `controlFor` and `resource.options()`. **The check runs through the caller's own accessor and that is the permission story rather than an optimisation** — `ctx.tables[accessor]` is the sibling model at this client's flavour, so the set a caller sees is the set their own `@@allow` shows them and the refusal reads *theirs is not in TaskTag*, which is the true statement from where they stand; `open` creates through that same accessor, so the source model's `@@gate`/`@@allow` answer who may extend the list. Written against `asSystem()` it would have offered every row to everybody, and it would have passed every test using one principal — both directions are asserted for that reason. **`suggested` issues no query at all**, asserted by a set whose source is `@@gate("8")` and unreadable: enforcing nothing has to cost nothing or nobody uses the strength that keeps the list travelling. **Six write paths carry a payload and all six call it**, and the grid derives that list from `client.js` itself rather than restating it, because a seventh added later would be silent. Client side, a bound column is asked BEFORE its foreign key — a bound FK is both and the set is the narrower answer, carrying the scope and the display column where the relation carries neither. `required` renders a picker, `open` and `suggested` render the same combobox (what separates them is what the SERVER does with a new value, not what a caller may type), a bound array renders a multiselect where an unbound one is still `json`. The declared `@@scope` **travels as a filter** — `$checkWhere` validates one, measured — so the offered list is the list the Data boundary will accept; a declared `where` cannot (`FJS-430`). | <a id="fjs-407"></a>FJS-407 | litestone — **a `@generated` column was invisible to the migration diff, so adding one to a live app emitted nothing at all** | 2026-08-22 | `introspect()` read `PRAGMA table_info`, and **`table_info` omits generated columns** — measured: on a table with two of them it returns `a` and nothing else, where `table_xinfo` returns `a(hidden=0), v(hidden=2), s(hidden=3)`. Both sides of the diff were built through that one function, so pristine and live agreed the column did not exist and the generator emitted an EMPTY migration: the schema declared a column, the database never got one, and nothing said so. Adding, dropping, changing the storage and changing the expression were all equally invisible. `introspect` now reads `table_xinfo` and carries a `generated: { mode, expr }` per column; **the pragma is the authority on WHETHER a column is generated and the CREATE statement is the only place the expression lives**, so the expression is parsed off `sqlite_master` (`parseGeneratedColumns`, a depth-and-quote walk rather than a regex — an expression may hold a comma, a paren and a quoted string) and a name the parse missed answers `null`, which the diff treats as *cannot judge* rather than as *changed*. A false positive in that parse can do no harm by construction: only columns the pragma has already flagged are ever looked up. **The two storages part company at the ALTER**: SQLite refuses `ADD COLUMN` for a STORED generated column on a populated table (`cannot add a STORED column`, measured — an empty one is allowed, which is why nothing seeded caught this), so a STORED add forces the rebuild `FJS-406` had just made safe, while a VIRTUAL add is an ordinary `ADD COLUMN` **carrying its `GENERATED ALWAYS AS` clause** — emitting the bare column would have applied cleanly and left a plain, writable column of the same name. Storage change and expression change are `modified`, hence a rebuild, since no ALTER reaches either. `litestone introspect` was fixed in the same pass: those columns were absent from its output before and would now have been emitted bare, which says a generated column is writable — it emits `@generated("…", stored)`, converting `"col"` back to `{col}` (the inverse of the parser's own expansion), and hands over an expression that cannot be spelled in that string as a `/// FIXME` line rather than mangling it. 10 tests, including an in-sync case so a fix that always saw a difference cannot pass. | [migrate.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) · [introspect.js](packages/litestone/src/tools/introspect.js) · [generated-rebuild.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/generated-rebuild.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-406"></a>FJS-406 | litestone — **a table rebuild listed its `@generated` columns in the copy, so the migration could not apply** | 2026-08-22 | **Renumbered from FJS-400**, which a concurrent session had issued for something else.  `INSERT INTO "customer__new" (…, "fullName") SELECT …, "fullName" FROM "customer"` — SQLite answers `cannot INSERT into generated column`, the runner rolls the whole migration back (one transaction per migration, ROLLBACK on failure), and the schema change is simply impossible to apply. **Not only about adding one**: the copy list came from every stored field, so a table that has carried a generated column for a year hits it the moment anything else about that table forces a rebuild — a dropped column, a changed type — which is the commoner case. Excluded now by KIND (`generated` and `funcCall`, the two attributes that emit `GENERATED ALWAYS AS`) rather than by being newly added, because the failure is a property of the column and not of one diff. Nothing is lost by not copying it: the rebuilt table computes it from the columns that were. `@derived` needs no exception — `isStoredField` already excludes it, since it has no column at all. Found while checking whether `@@label(fullName)` over a generated column was viable; the emitted SQL was read rather than trusted, and a first pass at applying it used a hand-rolled statement splitter that dropped the `CREATE TABLE` (it begins with a comment line) and nearly produced a different, wrong diagnosis. 4 tests. | [migrate.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) · [generated-rebuild.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/generated-rebuild.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-397"></a>FJS-397 | ui — **`MultiSelect` reached parity with the component it replaced** | 2026-08-22 | Four gaps, all closed. **(1) ranked matching** — both this and `CommandPalette` now rank through `@frontierjs/toolbelt/search`; the palette had no ordering at all, so typing a command's own name could leave it below others that merely mention the word. **(2) highlighting** — the matched run is marked through `segments()` and the component's own elements, never an HTML string, so a label out of a database row cannot inject; ranges are per FIELD, because a flat list marks the label at offsets belonging to the sub line. **(3) the >N collapse** — `collapseAfter={n}`, off by default because collapsing changes rendering for every caller that already has one on a screen; the chip is a `<button>`, being the only route back to the tokens. **(4) granular events** — `onadd` / `onremove` / `oncreate` beside `onchange`, which alone could say the selection is now X and never what MOVED. **The ordering is the substance and the test found it wrong**: the first implementation fired `onchange` from `commitValue` before the granular events, so a listener persisting an `allowNew` value was handed a changed selection naming a row the server had never heard of — `commitValue(next, announce)` now announces what moved, then what the selection is. `clearAll` fires `onremove` per value, so *this value left* stays true exactly when the most values leave at once. **Out of scope by decision, not omission**: VirtualList (Mesa has native support and it belongs there), and the `<option>` slot as a source (it rode a hidden native `<select>`, and `<Form>` is `novalidate` with `nativeValidationGuard`, so native participation is decided against here).
