| <a id="fjs-367"></a>FJS-367 | junction — **the shipped `paginate()` hook was inert in every app, and the fix is that it stops publishing a second copy of the page** | 2026-08-21 | Measured before and after. Before: `GET /things?$limit=5&$offset=10` through a service carrying `before: { find: [paginate(20, 100)] }` answered 200 with `ctx.locals.paginate = {"limit":20,"offset":0}` — the caller's page size silently ignored, on every request, for the life of the hook. `$` is transport syntax and the bridge is its one owner (Invariant 10): `splitParams` moves every `$` key onto `ctx.directives`, so `ctx.query.$limit` is never there past it. **Junction had already fixed this exact class once** — the comment above the split names `$limit / $offset / $orderBy / $select` as having been *"silently inert"* — and its own hook was not in that sweep; a grep for `$` reads off `ctx.query` now finds only `parseQuery`'s documented pre-directives fallback. **The fix is not "read directives instead", because that leaves the cause.** The hook NARROWS `ctx.directives` rather than minting `ctx.locals.paginate` beside it: two homes for one fact is what drifted, and narrowing is also what makes a ceiling reach anything, since a custom `find` handing `ctx.directives` to Litestone now gets it without threading a second value. `ctx.locals.paginate` is still written for the callers that read it, and is now typed as the same `Page` rather than a third spelling. **`clampPage` in `core/directives.ts` is the one answer to what a limit means** — the module imports nothing, and `parseQuery` calls it too: the hook used `parseInt` + `Math.min`, so `$limit=abc` gave it `Math.min(NaN, 100)` where the same request through a model service gave the default. A limit of 0 survives, being how a caller asks for the count alone. Worth knowing for anyone reading the old row: a MODEL service was never affected — `paginate: { default, max }` is a service option threaded into `parseQuery` correctly, which is why the breakage survived. Six tests in `tests/index.test.ts`; 1243 junction tests pass | 2026-08-21 | [hooks-builtin.ts](packages/junction/src/core/hooks-builtin.ts) · [core/directives.ts § clampPage](packages/junction/src/core/directives.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-370"></a>FJS-370 | junction — **`ctx.locals.db` was declared and still unusable: every model accessor typed `unknown`** | 2026-08-21 | **Filed with the wrong cause and corrected by probing it.** The row said junction installs `ctx.locals.db` and declares nothing; it does declare it, in the litestone adapter's own module augmentation, and has all along. What a compile actually said: `ctx.locals.db?.asSystem()` typechecks, `ctx.auth.user?.userId` typechecks, and **`ctx.locals.db?.post.findMany({})` is `unknown`** — which is every actual data call. The client interface carried `[model: string]: unknown` beside a fully-written `LitestoneTable` the index signature never used, so an app got the `$`-methods typed, wrote `dbOf(ctx): any` to do any work at all, and lost the whole client's safety with it (basecamp's is imported by 22 modules). **The shape of the fix is the reason it stayed broken**: an interface's declared members must satisfy its own index signature, and `asSystem(): LitestoneClient` is not a table — so one interface could only ever say `unknown`. Split into `LitestoneClientApi` and intersected with `{ [model: string]: LitestoneTable }`, which the rule does not apply to. `LitestoneTable | unknown` is not the fix and looks like it: a union with `unknown` collapses to `unknown`, the same trap [FJS-034](#fjs-034) hit on `ServiceDefinition`. **The cost is stated where it is paid**: any accessor name now resolves, so `db.pots.findMany()` compiles — traded knowing a Litestone client THROWS on an unknown property, so a typo is loud on the first call, and an app wanting it at compile time generates types and augments. `tests/context-db-types.ts` is compiled rather than run, so a regression is a `bun run typecheck` failure | 2026-08-21 | [core/litestone.ts](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [tests/context-db-types.ts](packages/junction/tests/context-db-types.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-368"></a>FJS-368 | litestone · junction | **A patch whose only key is `version` is a real write: it bumps the version and makes every other open editor of that row stale for a change nobody made.** Measured against a real client — `model Doc { id Int @id  title String  version Int @version }`, create, then `update({ where: { id: 1 }, data: { version: 1 } })` — answered `version: 2` with no other column touched. The value is a PRECONDITION the client sends back (`x-version`, `createResource` puts it on the next patch), not a value to write, so a form submitted with nothing edited becomes a write, and the stale-write machinery then correctly reports a conflict to everyone else. basecamp carries `changesNothing()` for exactly this and it is the only app that has noticed; every app using `@version` behind a generated `<Form>` has the behaviour. **The question is which boundary answers**: litestone could treat a version-only patch as a no-op (returning the row unchanged), or junction could drop it before the write, and the two differ for a caller who meant to touch the row's `updatedAt`. Whichever is chosen, the current answer — a real write, silently — is the one nobody wants | open | 2026-08-21 | [basecamp core/resource.ts § changesNothing](packages/basecamp/api/src/core/resource.ts) · `CLAUDE.md` § Bridge index `x-version` |
| <a id="fjs-369"></a>FJS-369 | junction | **There is no service-level audit trail, so an app that wants one re-derives junction's own announce rule.** `@@log(audit)` records a WRITE and `db.$audit()` records an EVENT; neither answers *what did this CALL do*, which is the question an operator asks — and the verbs that matter are custom methods (drain, deploy, cancel, trigger), which no write-level trail names. basecamp's answer is **193 lines** in `core/hooks.ts`: a `before: { all }` pre-image hook and an `after: { all }` writer, sharing one `recordable()` that **restates junction's own dispatch predicate** — everything except `find`/`get`, opting out on `ctx.dispatch === false`. Restating a rule the framework owns is the tell: `callService` is the single announcement point and it already decides this. What basecamp got right is what a framework version needs and what it had to reach for by hand — the model off `app.services` (the REGISTRY, not `app.service(name)`, which carries no `model` and produced a diff-less row for every event until it was found), `db.$protectedFields(accessor)` for redaction rather than a list of names, both sides re-read through the system client because a service may answer a projection and a scoped read strips protected columns. A plugin owning this would be small, and every app that needs a trail today writes the 193 lines or ships without one | open | 2026-08-21 | [basecamp core/hooks.ts § The application audit trail](packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts) · [FJS-154](#fjs-154) · [core/service.ts](packages/junction/src/core/service.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-366"></a>FJS-366 | junction — **Every WebSocket connection was evicted after 30s, in every app, because the keepalive had a server half and no client half** | 2026-08-20 | The channels plugin evicted any connection that had not sent `{type:'ping'}` in 30s, sweeping every 10s. `JunctionClient.startHeartbeat()` sends that ping and **nothing called it** — not junction, not sierra, not `fli new`'s scaffold — so a fresh app opened a socket, received `{type:'connected'}`, and was closed with `connection evicted` ~35s later, forever. Reported from a scaffolded app; measured there as a 39.4s close and a reconnect ~1s after each. Invisible unless watched, because the socket carried no event and the reconnect succeeded: a live list simply never updated. Two things were wrong under it. **Only a ping refreshed liveness**, so a client in the middle of service calls over that same socket was evicted while plainly alive. And **the client's default interval was 30s against a 30s timeout**, so calling it would have raced the reaper anyway. Fixed by moving the drive to the SERVER — it pings an idle socket every 15s and evicts one silent for 40s, and any frame counts as liveness, malformed ones included. The client answers from its message handler rather than a timer, which is what keeps a backgrounded tab connected: browsers throttle timers to ~1/min in a hidden tab, slower than any eviction window, and a message handler is not throttled. `channels(setup, { heartbeatInterval, heartbeatTimeout })` are the knobs; `startHeartbeat()` stays for a client whose server predates this, now defaulting to 15s. `tests/heartbeat.test.ts` runs the loop against a real socket at 100ms/400ms — idle is pinged, silent is evicted, pongs hold it, ordinary traffic holds it, and the shipped client holds it with no call by the app |
| <a id="fjs-364"></a>FJS-364 | cli — **`fli admin:generate` wrote one `resources/admin.mesa` holding every model, and declared a second Resource over services the app already had** | 2026-08-19 | Two defects under one file. The rule half: `admin.mesa` is N Resources under a name no model has, refused by `resource-file-name` and `resource-one-per-file` — measured on a two-model fixture, both errors, both Invariant 19. The half that actually breaks an app: it called `createResource('users')` beside the app's own `resources/User.mesa`, which is **two stores over one service** with a live socket on each — a write through the admin leaves the app's list stale and the app's write leaves the admin's, and nothing anywhere says why. Both close together. The model index moved into the layout's `<script module>` (`admin/_module.mesa` exports `models`, the dashboard imports it from there) — a layout is already this surface's own module, and module exports are importable by any other module (Mesa VISION §11, rule 30). The admin now imports the app's own Resource per model and writes one only where none exists, from `fli make:resource`'s template, **never overwriting, `--force` included** — force is about the generated pages, which are disposable. **Both halves of an existing Resource are READ rather than guessed**, and the second half was found by running it: the export name (`Person.mesa` exports `people`, which no plural rule applied to `Person` finds) *and* the service string, without which the irregular generated `/admin/persons/` over a service called `people` — the data worked and the URL, the route folder and the `make:service` hint were wrong together. A resource file exporting no `createResource` is named and its model skipped, rather than generating a page whose import resolves to `undefined`. Verified end to end on a three-model fixture: hand-written `User.mesa` byte-identical after `--force`, `fli check` clean where it previously reported two errors |
| <a id="fjs-363"></a>FJS-363 | cli — **Five scaffolds wrote or read a resource file the repo's own `fli check` refuses** | 2026-08-19 | Invariant 19 names a resource file for its MODEL — PascalCase, singular — and the export for its SERVICE. `make:resource` and `web:resource` built the path as `service + '.mesa'`, `make:model --resource` as `${plural}.mesa`, so `fli make:resource Order` wrote `orders.mesa` and the user's next `fli check` called it an error. The consumer half was wrong with it: `make:route`/`web:route --resource` generated a page importing `resources/${service}.mesa`, a file the fixed commands now never write — and its *does this resource exist yet* probe looked for `${service}.js`, the wrong name AND the wrong extension, so the warning fired on every run including the correct one. Only `make:scaffold` had it right (`${model}.mesa`), which is exactly why CI was green: the `scaffold` phase runs `fli scaffold` and reaches none of the five. Fixed by naming the file for the model in all five, with the split stated in a comment at each site, since the import line legitimately spells the two differently on either side of `from`. `fli validate`'s worked-example comment was stale the same way and is corrected. The sibling this does NOT fix is [FJS-364](#fjs-364) — `admin:generate`'s single `admin.mesa`, which is a shape question rather than a spelling |
| <a id="fjs-362"></a>FJS-362 | auth — **The test harness leaked every database it made** | 2026-08-19 | `removeAtExit` registered a `process.on('exit')` handler, and that handler **does not run under `bun test`** — probed with a one-test file that printed nothing from its hook and left its directory behind. So the suite leaked 23 directories per run from 2026-08-02: 2,093 of them, 748MB. It could not be an `afterAll` either, and the comment saying so is right — `@@log(audit)` flushes through the jsonl driver after the awaited call returns, so tearing down there raced it into `SQLITE_READONLY_DBMOVED`. The way out is to reap the PREVIOUS runs' directories on the way IN, where the owning process has provably exited however it exited, past a one-hour floor so a concurrent run of the same suite is never touched. One run took `/tmp` from 748MB to 19MB with 137 tests still green. The class is [FJS-361](#fjs-361) |
| <a id="fjs-359"></a>FJS-359 | junction — **Every 404 from a model service that did not declare `model:` said `undefined`** | 2026-08-19 | `createLitestoneBase` resolves its accessor with `model ?? ctx.service`, and six `NotFound` messages interpolated the bare option — which is `undefined` whenever a service relies on its filename, the default the autoloader is built around. So a scaffolded app answered `undefined with id=… not found` on every miss. One `modelLabel(ctx)` reading the same fallback |
| <a id="fjs-358"></a>FJS-358 | cli — **A scaffolded app had two auth files and ran the wrong one** | 2026-08-19 | `fli new` writes `api/src/core/auth.ts` and imports it from `api/src/app.ts`; it then composes `auth:install`, which writes `api/src/auth.ts` — a SECOND `createLitestoneAuth` over a SECOND `createClient` on the same SQLite file, imported by nothing. `project:new` carried a note admitting it. The dead file was the more complete one, which is how [FJS-357](#fjs-357) survived. `auth:install` detects the wired layout now and scaffolds nothing, and its next-steps named `api/src/server.ts` — a file that exists in no app this scaffold writes — while telling the reader to call `createApp({ auth })` a second time |
| <a id="fjs-357"></a>FJS-357 | cli — **An administrator in a scaffolded app was a plain user, and three of the schema's own access rules were dead** | 2026-08-19 | `api/src/core/db.ts` grades the gate on `isAdmin` and its own comment says the projection is made "where the session is built (auth's sessionFields)". `api/src/core/auth.ts` declared no `sessionFields`, and the `User` model ships a role STRING that auth stores and never interprets — so `isAdmin` was never on the session and nothing could ever grade above USER(4). Measured on a fresh scaffold with `role='admin'`: `DELETE /users/:id` → `403 "User.delete" requires level 5, user has level 4`, with no level any caller could reach; `PATCH` of another person's row → **404**, because `@@allow('update', id == auth().id \|\| auth().isAdmin)` matched nothing and a row policy hides rather than refuses; `PATCH {role:'admin'}` → **200 with the field silently stripped**, so an admin screen reports saved and nothing changed. One line, and the same probe now passes for an admin and still refuses a plain user in all three. `authCleanup` was constructed in the dead file and started nowhere, and `AUTH_SECRET` was generated into `.env` and validated by no `defineEnv` |
| <a id="fjs-356"></a>FJS-356 | sierra — **Every app that resolved sierra from npm was a blank screen** | 2026-08-19 | `optimizeDeps.exclude` named `'sierra'`, and the package is `@frontierjs/sierra`, so the exclusion matched nothing. Vite pre-bundled sierra, esbuild's scan died on the first `.mesa` it met, the entries were dropped from `_metadata.json` — and Vite still rewrote `virtual:sierra`'s imports to the `.vite/deps/` paths it had not written. Those 200 with the SPA fallback's HTML and an empty content type, which the browser refuses as a module: no router, no junction client, nothing on the page and one MIME-type line in the console. **Nothing here could see it.** An app in this repo resolves sierra to `packages/`, and Vite does not pre-bundle a linked dependency at all — the same blind spot as [FJS-251](#fjs-251) and [FJS-252](#fjs-252), and the reason the `scaffold` and `deploy` phases exist. Found by scaffolding through `create-frontier` against published packages and opening the result in a browser. Fixed by naming the package, and confirmed both ways: an installed app and a linked one now render, with a clean console |
| <a id="fjs-355"></a>FJS-355 | cli — **`fli keygen --env` printed the key it had just written** | 2026-08-19 | The stdout echo ran unconditionally, so `fli new --auth` put a real `ENCRYPTION_KEY` and a real `AUTH_SECRET` in terminal scrollback — and in the log of any CI job or scaffold that composes the command. The bare form exists to be piped, so the echo is kept where nothing else is carrying the key; `--env` and `--copy` have already delivered it, and `--print` is the way back for a caller that wants both. `auth:install` also lost the second success line it printed over keygen's own, and now declares each generated name in `.env.example`, which is the committed half — a key written only to `.env` is a key the next clone has no name for |
| <a id="fjs-354"></a>FJS-354 | cli — **`fli new --fli` created `cli/src/routes` and then `fli:init` refused it** | 2026-08-19 | The directory tree step pushed `cli/src/routes`, and `fli:init` — which exists to fill it — declines a path that already exists. | <a id="fjs-353"></a>FJS-353 | cli · sierra — **A fresh scaffold's own front page reported the working app as broken, three ways** | 2026-08-19 | The summary ended with `cp .env.example .env`, which overwrites the `.env` `auth:install` had just filled with both generated keys — an instruction that breaks the app it finished building; it is printed only when there is no `.env` now. The home page read `status.connected`, which is the SOCKET, and the client opens one only once it holds a token — so a visitor who had not signed in saw `connecting…` for ever; it asks `/api/health` instead and says separately that the socket opens at sign-in. And sierra's devtools toolbar injected by default while its only data source, junction's `devtools()` plugin, is opt-in — ten failed WebSocket retries against `ws://localhost:4000`, each a red line the browser writes itself and no page can suppress. Declaring the `devtools` block in `sierra.config.js` is the opt-in now |
| <a id="fjs-351"></a>FJS-351 | litestone — **A generated validation case did not isolate the rule it named, in both directions** | 2026-08-19 | `generateValidationCases` built every case from ONE attribute with no idea what else sat on the field. **A boundary claimed a value the field refused** — `@length(3, 200)` on an `@email` column produced `'xxx'`, so the write was refused and the runner reported *@length allows this value and the write was refused*. Measured on a six-field schema: 8 of 12 boundary cases false, 4 of 6 fields reported broken when nothing was, and the fix a reader reaches for is deleting a rule from the schema. **An invalid case was refused by somebody else's rule and counted as proof of its own** — `''` on that column is rejected by `@email`, so the two cases naming `@length` proved nothing about it. Measured by disabling `@length` in `validate.js`: on a single-rule field the runner says *the schema declares @length(3,20) and the write was ACCEPTED*, on the `@email` field it said **nothing**, and the only entries there were the two false alarms — which appear whether the rule is enforced or not, so the checker could not tell an enforced `@length` from a missing one, and the false alarm is what hid it. The same measurement now yields four findings, all naming `@length`, and zero on a schema with nothing wrong with it. `attempt()` also carried the message every case declared and never compared it; it does now, as a backstop for an implementation refusing for a reason the schema did not predict. Both closed against one judge: `validateField` is now exported from `core/validate.js` — the function that decides this on a real write — because a table of formats in the generator would be a second definition of every rule. **It is asked about the field's OTHER rules only**: whether a value satisfies or breaks the rule it NAMES is established by construction, and asking the implementation makes the runner its own oracle — the first version did, and disabling `@length` then reported *not checked* instead of *the write was ACCEPTED*, losing the finding the runner exists to produce. Caught by measuring rather than by reasoning. **The repair is format-blind**: it grows or trims the factory's own valid sample and asks the validators, so an email grows in front of its own domain and `@startsWith("ORD-")` keeps its prefix with no code here containing either word; shrinking works on alphanumeric RUNS and leaves punctuation alone, which turns `email1@example.com` into `e@e.c` at five. Which dimension is free depends on the rule — a boundary IS its length, an invalid `@length` case need only sit outside the bound, every other rule's length is incidental. What cannot be isolated is `uncheckable`: named, with the blocking rules' own messages, because a rule that stops being asked about is this runner's failure mode one layer up — and **`litestone mutate` excludes it from the kill count**, since a row generated from the original schema appears identically under every mutant, which is the shape the 22 `error` rows had when they scored a 14-mutant schema 93%. Five tests, and basecamp's `Invitation.email` carries `@email @length(6, 200)` again — 6 rather than 5 because `a@b.c` is the shortest string `@email` accepts, so a bound at the floor is unviolatable and the runner says so |
| <a id="fjs-032"></a>FJS-032 | basecamp — **No invitation flow: the setup wizard was the only door in for a human** | 2026-08-19 | `workspaces.addMember` takes a `userId`, so it could only reach somebody who already had an account, and the one route that makes one — `/auth/register` — leaves them with no account row and no workspace, after which every scoped request 400s and they cannot create a workspace either. Closed with `model Invitation`: an offer of membership to an EMAIL ADDRESS, carrying the workspace and the role across the gap where there is no user to hang them on. **The row IS the pending state** — no `acceptedAt`, no `revokedAt`; accepting writes a `WorkspaceMember` carrying `invitedBy`/`invitedAt` forward and deletes it, revoking deletes it, and `@@log(audit)` is what survives either way. Those three columns had been declared since the schema was written and nothing had ever written one. The token is `@guarded(all)`, so no scoped read can answer it and the link is shown ONCE, the shape an issued API key already had. **`invitations.preview` and `.accept` run with no session at all** and are exempt from `sessionScope` for the reason the population they serve is not a member yet and may not exist yet; everything a token cannot decide (unknown, expired, workspace gone, workspace suspended) is decided in one place because none of the hooks that normally decide it are running. An address that already has an account must be signed in as that account — not a password on this method, which would be a second login door with none of the first one's rate limiting and an oracle when it refuses — so `/login/` grew a same-origin `?next=`. **Mail goes through conduit** (`api/src/core/mailer.ts`): the provider is a declared target with a credential ref, and `app.mail` is ABSENT where none is configured, which is a supported state — the invitation still issues a working link and the screen says plainly that nothing was sent. Driven end to end in `web/test/verify.mjs` against a real mail sink on 8121, because the halves fail apart: 21 checks from *the link is shown once* through *a stranger with no account accepts and lands inside the app* to *the accepted link is dead* |
| <a id="fjs-352"></a>FJS-352 | basecamp — **`POST /workspaces` 400d for any caller that did not send its own slug** | 2026-08-19 | `create()` derived `data.slug ??= slugify(name)` in the METHOD, and `model:` puts `autoValidate` after the user hooks and before the method — so `slug is required` came back for a column the service derives. The same file already had `stampOwnership` in `before: { create }` for exactly this ordering, with `accountId` and `ownerId` in it; `slug` was the one left behind. Nothing caught it because the only caller was the browser, whose form sends a slug. Found by an API-level probe of the invitation flow that needed a second workspace |
| <a id="fjs-349"></a>FJS-349 | basecamp · conduit · junction — **The three endpoints an Outpost calls took no credential at all, and one of them redirected the fleet** | 2026-08-19 | Measured before and after, against a running API. Before: a POST carrying nothing but `X-Service-Method: heartbeat` answered **200**, moved a server `pending → online`, and registered the Conduit target `outpost:<serverId>` at `http://attacker.invalid:9999` — read back out of `conduit_targets` — which points every later `/exec`, `/deploy` and `/system/prune` for that machine at a host the caller owns, HMAC-signed with this app's own secret. The comment beside the sessionScope exemption said the transport verified a signature; **conduit signs what it SENDS and nothing checked what arrived**, in the whole repo. **The scheme now has one owner**: `@frontierjs/toolbelt/signature` — canonical string, sign and verify — read by conduit's transport (which lost its hand-rolled copy), by basecamp's `requireOutpostSignature`, and by the Outpost itself. **Junction had to grow one thing for this to be possible**: a signature is over BYTES, and a hook handed only the parsed object would have to re-serialise, which means both ends agreeing on key order and spacing forever — so `parseBody` keeps the text it already decoded and it reaches a hook as `ctx.$raw.rawBody`. Registered app-level rather than per service, so a new machine-facing method cannot inherit the sessionScope exemption without the signature. Fail-closed in every direction: no secret configured is a refusal, an in-process call with no `$raw` is a refusal, and the reason is logged and never returned. Proven five ways after: unsigned 401, signed 200, replay 401, signature moved to another path 401, body swapped under a good signature 401 — plus four tests in `api/test/services.test.ts`, eleven specs in toolbelt, and a drive check that an unsigned `cleanup.report` is refused. **One fleet secret, and the limit is stated in the code**: a compromised machine can forge another's check-in; per-server secrets need the mint and hand-over that ring 1 has not built |
| <a id="fjs-350"></a>FJS-350 | junction — **A test request fired on the microtask queue, so an `await` before `.send()` silently sent nothing** | 2026-08-19 | `request(app)`'s builder ended with `Promise.resolve().then(execute)`, which schedules the call the moment the builder is made. Any `await` between `.post(path)` and `.send(body)` therefore let it go out first — with no body, and without any header set after that point — and nothing said so: the request succeeded, the service saw `null`, and the test asserted against whatever that produced. Found writing the FJS-349 tests, where computing a signature is itself an await, so four correct tests failed and the natural next move is to doubt the code they were testing. Fixed by making it lazy — the promise is created by `then()`/`catch()` and memoised — and covered by a test that awaits between the two calls |
| <a id="fjs-066"></a>FJS-066 | cli — **No stack trace from a command named where in the `.md` it threw, and the Node one was confidently wrong** | 2026-08-19 | The `sourceURL` pragma relabelled the PATH in a frame and left the LINE alone, so Node reported `boom.md:15` for a throw on line 9 of an **11-line file** — a location that reads as authoritative and cannot exist — while Bun ignored the pragma and named a temp shim deleted on exit. Bun ignores an inline source map and a linked `.map` too; all four measured, so neither runtime was ever going to answer this. **fli answers it instead, and the map is ONE INTEGER**: `transformMarkdown` turns prose into `//` comments rather than dropping it, and `stripScriptBlocks` now leaves the block's own height behind in blank lines instead of cutting them — which collapses the two offsets a mid-file `<script>` used to need into one. `compileCliWithMap` returns `genLine - mdLine`; `core/stack.js` is the one owner of the rewrite (a string pass over `err.stack` and its `cause` chain, not `Error.prepareStackTrace` — that hook is global and V8-shaped, and taking it means re-implementing default formatting for every frame that is not ours, on two runtimes that format differently). The pragma is gone and the body is no longer indented into `run()`, so columns are the author's too. Both runtimes now say `boom.md:9`. **The test runs the command through `bin/fli.js` under node AND bun** — every unit-level expectation the pragma set was satisfied for its whole life, so only executing it could tell |
| <a id="fjs-031"></a>FJS-031 | basecamp — **Every service published after every method, reads included** | 2026-08-19 | A `GET` broadcast the row it had just read to every browser in the workspace, from `after: { all: [publishToChannels(workspaceChannel(app))] }` on all seventeen services. `all` means all, and no per-service hook list can exclude reads for itself — junction decides what to announce in `callService`, once, and that place excludes `find`/`get` **by name**, because a custom method that only reads is indistinguishable from one that writes at that layer. | <a id="fjs-154"></a>FJS-154 | basecamp — **The audit trail recorded that something happened, never what changed** | 2026-08-19 | `AuditEvent.diff` was `Json?` and nothing wrote it, so the trail could say a server was drained and not what state it was in. Which columns must not be written down comes from **`db.$protectedFields(accessor)`**, new in litestone — the third sibling of `$checkWhere`/`$checkOrderBy`, same contract, on every flavour of client — rather than a list of names in this app that goes stale the first time somebody adds a `@secret`. A protected field appears as `[redacted]` on both sides, because THAT it changed is the fact an operator needs. `updatedAt` is skipped (it moves on every write and says nothing), long text is cut, and Json columns are compared by value because identity reports every one of them as changed on every write. `/activity/` renders the fields with before → after behind a disclosure; two tests in `api/test/services.test.ts`, one check in the drive |

| <a id="fjs-337"></a>FJS-337 | junction · basecamp — **A service had no way to reserve a query key, so a documented `?workspace_id=` fallback was refused before anything read it** | 2026-08-18 | `$`-names are directives (Invariant 10) and everything else is graded against the model's columns, so there was no third answer: `autoFilter` answered *Unknown filter key 'workspace_id' — did you mean 'workspaceId'?* before the hook that reads it ran, and the app could not fix it from its own side either. **`reservedQuery: ['workspace_id']` on the service** is that third answer, and it is the query-side mirror of `@transient`: declared where it is owned, then LIFTED — the keys move to `ctx.reserved` and `ctx.query` is columns alone. | <a id="fjs-295"></a>FJS-295 | caravan — **A job handler that never returned held its slot forever and stalled the queue in silence** | 2026-08-18 | Three changes, separate on purpose. **`timeout`** on a handler, a job file or a queue bounds one attempt; absent means no bound, honestly, because a default would kill every legitimately long job in every app that upgraded. A queue-level default is resolved at REGISTRATION so `registrations()` reports the bound that will be enforced rather than the one the handler wrote. A timeout is an **ordinary failure** — it counts as an attempt and retries on the same ladder as a throw, rather than becoming a fifth status. **What it cannot do is stop the handler**, which the row named as the design question: nothing in JavaScript cancels a promise, so the abandoned invocation may still be writing while the retry runs. Delivery here was already at-least-once (`FJS-D35`) and this widens the window rather than opening it — both consequences are handled rather than left to chance, since its later rejection would otherwise be unhandled and take the process down, and its later SUCCESS is the most useful thing a person debugging this can be told (*the work you gave up on finished after 45 minutes*), so it is announced rather than swallowed. **`oldestRunningMs`** is the half for jobs nobody thought to bound, which is most of them: `running: 1` for the life of the process is exactly what a busy queue reports, and a count that never moves beside a climbing age is not — asked of the DATABASE, so another instance's stuck job is in the answer, and `null` rather than 0 where nothing runs. **`drainTimeout`** because a hardcoded 30s is the whole shutdown budget of a deployment whose SIGTERM grace is shorter. **Two whitelist bugs fell out**, the same shape twice: `defineJob` built its definition from a fixed key list and `autoload` re-listed a definition's keys back into `handle(name, fn, opts)`, so a `timeout` in a job file was accepted and dropped twice; autoload passes the definition WHOLE now (`JobRegistrar.handle(job)`), removing the third whitelist rather than adding a key to it. Found by [FJS-343](#fjs-343) printing `**none**` for a job that had just declared 30s — the artefact catching a defect the day after it was written, which is the case for `PHILOSOPHY.md` § *A projection must be legible* stated better than the argument for it did. A synchronous handler that never yields stays unreachable: the timer needs the event loop, same ground as `FJS-294`. 11 tests, four mutants killed | [worker.ts](packages/caravan/src/worker.ts) `_bounded` · [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts) `oldestRunning` · [autoload.ts](packages/caravan/src/autoload.ts) · [tests/timeout.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/timeout.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-339"></a>FJS-339 | mesa — **A snippet parameter that is a destructuring pattern compiled and could not work** | 2026-08-18 | The argument arrives as a getter, so `{#snippet row([name, q])}` emitted `(__anchor, [name, q])` and destructured the accessor FUNCTION — `TypeError: function is not iterable`, thrown from the compiled file naming no snippet, the whole enclosing block rendering nothing. **`{#each}`'s answer does not transfer**: a block re-runs per item and a snippet's DOM is built once, so unwrapping the argument at the top of the body would compile, run, and freeze every name at its first value — the frozen-argument bug the getters exist to prevent (VISION §9.5). So the destructuring moves into the READ: one getter, and each bound name compiled to its own path through it (`$$arg0()[0]`, `$$arg0().id`), which keeps a subscription per name. A default, a rest element or a nested pattern is refused by name, because none can be read lazily. **Fixing it uncovered the same trap one layer down** — `_isReactive`'s bare-call heuristic excludes `$`-prefixed identifiers, so the new reads were classed STATIC and written once: the first render passed and every reassignment was ignored, which is the frozen-argument bug arriving through its own fix. Caught by a browser spec asserting the reassignment rather than the first paint; basecamp's Hub carries the natural spelling again |

| <a id="fjs-346"></a>FJS-346 | junction · caravan — **What an app runs on a clock had no reader, so a schedule that stopped being registered was invisible** | 2026-08-18 | The first artefact written against `PHILOSOPHY.md` § *A projection must be legible*. Every other snapshot here records a surface someone reaches; this one records work that starts with no request, no caller and no screen — the part of an app whose failure is silent by construction, since a route that stops working is a 404 somebody sees and a schedule that stops firing is *nothing happening*, which is indistinguishable from nothing needing to happen ([FJS-327](#fjs-327), [FJS-328](#fjs-328)). `junction jobs --app <module>` reads a BUILT app for the same reason `junction surface` does: a job file registers itself by being autoloaded and a plugin registers timers of its own. **Two registries, kept apart on the page** (`FJS-D36`) — `app.jobs` is durable, `app.scheduler` is a bare in-process timer running in every replica rather than once across them — and **no queue installed renders differently from a queue with no handlers**, because one app does no background work and the other will dispatch into a queue with nothing to run it. | <a id="fjs-343"></a>FJS-343 | cli — **`fli new --auth` could not finish: `auth:install` handed its `litestone db push` an EMPTY `ENCRYPTION_KEY`** | 2026-08-18 | Two things stacked, and either alone is enough. Step 6 existed to fix exactly that by assigning `process.env.ENCRYPTION_KEY` before the push, and **under Bun that assignment does not reach a child at all**: `child_process` hands over the environment the process STARTED with, where node would pass the mutation. Measured directly — `bun -e "process.env.FOO='x'; execSync('printenv FOO')"` prints nothing. Bun is the only runtime `fli` runs on, so the fix had been a no-op since it was written. The adopted values are passed as `env:` on the exec now. **Invisible for two further reasons**, both fixed in the same session: `fli new` caught the failure, warned, and printed `✓ created` anyway, and `runFli` shelled out to a GLOBAL `fli` on PATH, so the tree's copy of this command had never run in a scaffold at all. The code did the opposite. `export let label = ''` made "not stated" and "deliberately blank" the same value, and `label={label \|\| undefined}` then collapsed the blank one back to "not stated", so `Field` fell through to `nameToLabel(name)` and drew a label the caller had just turned off. `Field` itself was right all along — `<Field label="">` renders no label row — so only the pass-through was broken. **A control given a `name` therefore always had a visible label**, which is the shorthand `<Form>` wants and is wrong everywhere else: basecamp's server filter bar has three controls, only the Input needs a `name` (its submit handler reads `e.target.search.value`), and the bar rendered one labelled control 38px tall beside two unlabelled ones — with a *second* label, since the caller had already supplied a `visually-hidden` one. Fixed across all thirteen: the default is `undefined`, `''` is passed through. Measured before and after with `renderComponent`; the kit's 667 browser assertions are unchanged |
| <a id="fjs-342"></a>FJS-342 | toolbelt · junction · caravan — **Four mechanisms answered *has this already happened?* and each built its key at its call site; two of them interpolated caller-supplied text into it unescaped** | 2026-08-18 | The occurrence key is not internal: a stated dispatch id is the jobs table's PRIMARY KEY, which is what makes a replayed dispatch a no-op rather than a second row. So a key that can collide is two units of work silently becoming one. `@frontierjs/toolbelt/history` is the one definition — `occurrenceKey(kind, ...parts)` — and the property it exists for is **injectivity**: `%` is escaped before `:`, because escaping only the separator makes a part reading `%3A` and a part holding a real colon encode to the same bytes; `null`/`undefined` are refused rather than stringified, since `cron:daily:undefined` is one key every fire of that job shares. Two latent collisions closed with it. **Junction's idempotency key** joined four parts and two are outside its control — the header a caller writes and a principal id the auth provider issues — so a principal of `user-1:a` with key `b` and a principal of `user-1` with key `a:b` were one cache entry, which is one caller replayed another's answer. **Caravan's cron fire** interpolated a job name, so `report:daily` at minute 5 and `report` at `daily:5` were one key and the second never ran. Both are byte-identical for input without a `:`. **The outbox id MOVED**, deliberately: it dispatched under the bare row id into a table shared with every caller-stated id, so outbox row 7 and a caller's `7` were one primary key — it is `outbox:<id>` now, and that is a format change undelivered rows cannot survive, which costs nothing while no queue has rows in it and cannot be done free after a deploy. | <a id="fjs-341"></a>FJS-341 | sierra · junction · litestone — **A live store defeated `@version` on any screen holding a draft: the other writer's change was erased with the guard in place and no error anywhere** | 2026-08-18 | `createResource` remembered a version off the STORE, which was the right answer to the wrong question — a WS push reaches the store as an upsert and never passes through a call result, so without it a row a second tab patched left a pre-patch number behind. The cost was the other half: a push moves the number and moves nothing the person is looking at, so a save from a screen holding a DRAFT carried a revision nobody there had read and won the race the column exists to lose. Measured in basecamp. **The version is now recorded from READS this resource performed** — every call result, and a load that was not superseded — and never from the store. `load()` carries its own stamp for that, because a store notification has no provenance: a `set()` from a winning load and an `upsert()` from a push arrive as the same event, and junction's own stamp (`FJS-082`) governs the store rather than this. | <a id="fjs-252"></a>FJS-252 | repo — **The scaffold `fli new` writes is authored against HEAD and installs the published packages, and nothing compares the two** | 2026-08-18 | Two checks, and then a measurement in both directions.
